# Chapter 1: Forces

## Key Questions
* What is a force?
* What effect can a force have on an object?
* Do forces have to be between objects which are touching?

---

## 1.1 Types of Forces

### What is a force?
A force is a push or a pull on an object. When you move a heavy object, such as a desk, you must either push it or pull it to change its position. By doing this, you are exerting a force on the object.

<!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram -->

### Key Vocabulary
* **Force:** A push or a pull exerted on an object.
* **Net force:** The sum of all forces acting on an object.
* **Contact force:** A force that acts only when objects are physically touching.
* **Non-contact force:** A force that acts over a distance without physical contact.
* **Newton (N):** The SI unit of force.

### Did You Know?
The **newton (N)** is named after Sir Isaac Newton, an English physicist and mathematician recognized for his work in mechanics and his three laws of motion.

The newton is defined as the force required to accelerate a mass of $1\text{ kg}$ at a rate of $1\text{ m/s}^2$. This relationship is expressed by the formula:

$$F = m \times a$$

Where:
* $F$ is the force in newtons ($\text{N}$)
* $m$ is the mass in kilograms ($\text{kg}$)
* $a$ is the acceleration in metres per second squared ($\text{m/s}^2$)

<!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram -->

---

# Chapter 1: Forces

## Introduction
Forces are an integral part of our daily lives. We rely on forces for basic human movement, such as our muscles pulling on our bones to facilitate walking. We also use forces to interact with our environment, such as opening doors or picking up food.

---

## Activity: What can forces do?

### Objective
To experiment with different objects and observe the effects of pushing and pulling. You will observe how forces can cause acceleration in solid objects and how they can distort the shape of flexible materials.

### Materials Required
*   Blocks (wooden or metal)
*   Sponge or piece of foam
*   Ball
*   Blown up balloon
*   Putty or play dough

### Instructions
Work in groups of 2 or 3 to perform the following tasks and record your observations:

1.  **Pushing a stationary object:** Place the ball on the ground and push it towards your partner. Describe what happens to the ball.
2.  **Changing direction:** While the ball is moving, have a third person push the ball at an angle to its current path. Describe the change in the ball's movement.
3.  **Opposing force:** Exert a force in the opposite direction to the ball's movement while it is already moving. What happens to the ball?
4.  **Assisting force:** Exert a force in the same direction as the ball's movement while it is already moving. What happens to the ball?

## Did You Know?
In 1687, Isaac Newton published *Philosophiæ Naturalis Principia Mathematica*. This work is considered one of the most important books in the history of science. It describes the theory of universal gravitation and the three laws of motion, which form the foundation of classical mechanics.

### Practical Activities: Investigating Forces

6. **Experiment with Putty and Balloons:**
   * Apply pulling or pushing forces to a piece of putty (or play dough) and a blown-up balloon.
   * **Observation:** Note how these forces change the shape of the objects.

7. **Experiment with Wooden Blocks:**
   * Apply a push or pull to wooden blocks.
   * **Observation:** Determine if you are able to change their motion and if you are able to change their shape.

8. **Experiment with a Sponge:**
   * Twist a piece of sponge. This is a type of force that changes the object's shape.
   * Press the sponge between both hands. This specific action is called **compression**.

---

### Effects of Forces

Forces can have the following effects on objects:

*   **Deformation:** Forces can change the **shape** of an object.
*   **Change in Motion:** Forces can change the **motion** of an object.
    *   If an object is stationary, a force can cause it to start moving.
    *   If an object is already moving, a force can cause it to speed up or slow down.
*   **Change in Direction:** Forces can change the **direction** in which an object is moving.

For CAPS Physical Sciences and Geography learners studying the universe, this visual conveys the composition of stellar systems, contrasting the distinct locations of blue supergiants like Rigel and red supergiants like Betelgeuse relative to star-forming regions such nebulae.



---

### Motion and Velocity

**Key Definitions:**
*   **Speed:** Describes how fast an object is moving.
*   **Velocity:** The rate of change of the position of an object. It describes both how fast an object is moving and the direction in which it is moving.

**Constant Velocity:**
An object moves at a constant velocity if it travels at the same speed in the same direction.
*   *Example:* A car travelling along a highway at $100\text{ km/h}$ in a straight line.

---

### Acceleration

**Definition:**
Acceleration is the rate of change of a body's velocity with time. It is a measure of how an object's speed changes every second.

*   **Note:** When a force is applied to an object, it can cause the object to speed up or slow down. This change in motion is defined as acceleration.

---

### New Words Summary
*   **Speed:** The rate at which an object covers distance.
*   **Velocity:** Speed in a given direction.
*   **Acceleration:** The rate of change of velocity.
*   **Deformation:** The action or process of changing the shape of an object.

# Activity: Is it a push or a pull?

## Instructions
1. Look at the pictures in the table below.
2. Describe the action in each image.
3. Decide if the force being exerted is a **push** or a **pull**.
4. Describe the **effect** of the force (e.g., does it change the shape, speed, or direction of the object?).



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0003-00.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0003-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0003-00.png]**
> This educational graphic is a composite astrophotography image from the Hubble Space Telescope (HST WFPC2) displaying four distinct protoplanetary disks (proplyds) within the Orion Nebula. Visible features include central luminous protostars surrounded by dark, dusty circumstellar disks silhouetted against glowing nebular gas. Conceptually, this visual supports the CAPS Earth and Space Sciences curriculum by providing empirical evidence for the nebular hypothesis, demonstrating how solar systems and planetary disks form around young stars.



| Action | Push or pull? | Effect |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Kicking a ball | | |
| Moulding clay | | |
| Playing with a toy wagon | | |

***

### Key Concepts
*   **Force:** A push or a pull acting on an object.
*   **Push:** A force that moves an object away from the source of the force.
*   **Pull:** A force that moves an object towards the source of the force.
*   **Effect of Force:** Forces can cause objects to:
    *   Start moving or stop moving.
    *   Change speed (speed up or slow down).
    *   Change direction.
    *   Change shape (deform).

### Understanding Forces: Pushes and Pulls

Forces are interactions between objects that can cause a change in motion or shape. Every force involves an **agent** (the object applying the force) and an **object** (the object experiencing the force).

#### Activity: Identifying Forces

| Action | Push or Pull? | Effect |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 

![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0004-00.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0004-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0004-00.png]**
> This astronomical composite image displays a deep-space nebula with glowing red filaments arching upward against a greenish-hued galactic background and dark interstellar dust lanes. The visual features contrasting emission nebulae, glowing gas plumes, and distant pinpoint stars without any overlaid text labels, axes, or arrows. Conceptually, it supports CAPS Physical Sciences and Geography outcomes by illustrating stellar evolution, interstellar matter, emission spectra of heated gases, and the energetic processes occurring within galaxies.

 A rocket blasting off into space. | Push | The rocket moves upwards against gravity. |
| 

![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0004-02.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0004-02.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0004-02.png]**
> This astronomical image displays a deep-space nebula filled with interstellar gas, cosmic dust clouds, and numerous bright stars forming clusters. There are no explicit text labels, axes, or arrows visible in the composite photograph. Conceptually, it illustrates stellar nurseries and the birth of stars within nebulae, supporting CAPS Earth and Beyond topics regarding the life cycle of stars and the origin of the universe.

 Exercising on a bar. | Pull | The person lifts their body weight upwards. |

---

#### Questions

**1. In the example of the girl kicking the ball, which is the object experiencing the force and which is the agent of the force?**
*   **Object experiencing the force:** The ball.
*   **Agent of the force:** The girl (her foot).

**2. When moulding clay, which object is experiencing the force and which object is the agent?**
*   **Object experiencing the force:** The clay.
*   **Agent of the force:** The person's hands.

---

### Pairs of Forces

In physics, forces do not exist in isolation. When one object exerts a force on a second object, the second object simultaneously exerts a force of equal magnitude and opposite direction back on the first object. This concept is fundamental to understanding how objects interact in the physical world.

# ACTIVITY: Pairs of Forces

## Instructions

1. **Wall Push:** Work in groups of three. Go to your classroom wall and push against it. Describe what you feel.
2. **Wall Reaction:** When you push on the wall, do you think the wall is pushing back on your hands? How does this force compare to the force you are exerting on the wall?
3. **Triangle Pull:** Stand in a triangle with your two partners and hold hands. Pull on each others' hands. Do you feel your partners' hands pulling back as you pull?
4. **Triangle Push:** Still standing in a triangle, place your palms up against each other and push against each others' hands. Do you feel your partners' hands pushing back as you push?
5. **Shoulder Push:** Stand shoulder-to-shoulder with your two partners. The two learners on the outside must push against the shoulders of the learner in the middle.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0005-04.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0005-04.png)

The visible elements include a glass conical flask filled with blue liquid, a sketched purple underwater ecosystem containing aquatic life, a surface vessel with an air tube connected to a diver, and rising bubbles leading to an empty thought bubble above the flask. Conceptually, this visual encourages CAPS Physical Sciences and Natural Sciences learners to connect laboratory chemistry (chemical reactions producing gas bubbles) with macroscopic phenomena, fostering imaginative and contextualized scientific thinking.



6. **Equal Force:** What happens when you both push with the same force?
7. **Unequal Force:** What happens when one of you pushes with a harder force than the other?

### Activity: Investigating Forces in Pairs

**Instructions:**
8. The learner in the middle must stretch out his or her arms. The learners on the outside must pull on the middle learner's hands in opposite directions.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0006-00.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0006-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0006-00.png]**
> This educational infographic displays a horizontal sequence of five glowing stellar spheres against a starry cosmic background, titled "The temperature of a star determines its colour". Below each sphere are the visible colour labels: "Blue-white", "White", "Yellow", "Orange", and "Red". Conceptually, the diagram helps high school learners understand the relationship between stellar surface temperature and emitted light, showing that hotter stars radiate shorter wavelengths appearing blue-white while cooler stars radiate longer wavelengths appearing red.



**Questions for Investigation:**
9. What happens when you both pull with equal force?
10. What happens when one of you pulls with a stronger force than the other?
11. What happens when one of you pulls and the other pushes?

### Key Concept: Newton's Third Law of Motion

**DID YOU KNOW?**
Newton's Third Law of Motion states that when one body exerts a force on a second body, the second body simultaneously exerts a force equal in strength and opposite in direction to that of the first body.

#### Understanding Action and Reaction
Whenever one object exerts a force on a second object, the second object exerts a force back on the first object. We say that forces act in pairs. 

*   **Action:** The initial force exerted by the first object.
*   **Reaction:** The force exerted back by the second object.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0006-02.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0006-02.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0006-02.png]**
> This educational graphic is an astronomical scale comparison diagram depicting four spherical celestial bodies of vastly different sizes against a dark space background. It features a tiny red point source in the bottom left, a medium yellow star, a large light-blue giant star, and an immense dark-blue supergiant structure dominating the upper background. Aligned in a sequence of increasing magnitude, this visual conveys the concept of stellar size classification and relative scale, helping high school learners understand the immense diversity in the radii and volumes of stars across the universe.



**Summary of Observations:**
When you exert a force on a wall, you experience the wall exerting a force back on you. These forces always act in pairs on different objects and are:
1.  **Equal** in strength.
2.  **Opposite** in direction.

# Understanding Net and Resultant Forces

When multiple forces act on an object simultaneously, their combined effect determines the object's motion. This combined effect is known as the **net force** (or overall force).

## 1. Core Concepts

### Balanced Forces
When two forces are equal in magnitude but opposite in direction, they cancel each other out.
*   **Net Force:** $0\text{ N}$
*   **Result:** The object remains in its state of motion (stationary or constant velocity).

### Unbalanced Forces (Resultant Force)
When forces acting on an object are not equal, or are acting in the same direction, they do not cancel out. The remaining force is called the **resultant force**.
*   **Opposite directions:** If one force is larger than the other, the resultant force acts in the direction of the larger force.
*   **Same direction:** If forces act in the same direction, they add together to produce a larger overall net force.

## 2. Worked Example: Tug-of-War
In a tug-of-war, two groups pull on a rope in opposite directions.

| Scenario | Force A (Left) | Force B (Right) | Net Force | Result |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Balanced** | $500\text{ N}$ | $500\text{ N}$ | $0\text{ N}$ | Rope remains stationary |
| **Unbalanced** | $400\text{ N}$ | $600\text{ N}$ | $200\text{ N}$ (Right) | Rope moves to the right |

## 3. Representing Forces
To analyze forces acting on a body, we use diagrams. The standard convention for representing forces is:
1.  **Arrows:** Use arrows to represent the magnitude and direction of the force.
2.  **Point of Application:** Always draw the arrow starting from the **centre** of the object on which the force is acting.

### Summary of Force Calculations
If we define the right direction as positive ($+$) and the left direction as negative ($-$), the net force ($F_{net}$) can be calculated as:

$$F_{net} = F_1 + F_2 + ... + F_n$$

*   If $F_{net} = 0\text{ N}$, the forces are **balanced**.
*   If $F_{net} \neq 0\text{ N}$, the forces are **unbalanced**, resulting in a **resultant force**.

### Representing Forces

Forces are represented using arrows. The characteristics of the arrow indicate the nature of the force:
*   **Direction:** The direction in which the arrow points shows the direction of the force.
*   **Magnitude (Size):** The length of the arrow indicates the size of the force. A short arrow represents a small force, while a long arrow represents a large force.

The diagram serves as an online extension prompt directing students to digital resources regarding stellar characteristics. Conceptually, it engages learners studying Earth and Beyond in CAPS Natural Sciences by connecting planetary observation on Earth to the broader astrophysical study of star temperatures and colours.

### Multiple Forces and Net Force

When more than one force acts on an object, the overall effect depends on the magnitude and direction of each individual force.

#### Worked Example: Calculating Net Force
In a scenario where Jabu pulls Rod with a force of $10\text{ N}$ to the left, and Viantha pulls Rod with a force of $6\text{ N}$ to the right:

> **TAKE NOTE:** When forces act in opposite directions, one direction is assigned a positive value and the other a negative value. In this example, the force exerted by Viantha is given a negative value because it is in the opposite direction to the force exerted by Jabu.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0008-04.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0008-04.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0008-04.png]**
> This astronomical photograph displays a dense, spheroidal cluster of thousands of stars bound together by gravity, known as a globular cluster. The image features a bright, concentrated central core densely packed with luminous stars, which gradually disperses outward into a vast halo of individual stellar points against the black background of space. For CAPS Physical Sciences learners studying Earth and Beyond, this visual conveys the structure and formation of large stellar systems within our universe, demonstrating how gravitational attraction shapes galactic morphology and stellar distribution.



#### Free-Body Diagrams
A **free-body diagram** is a simplified representation where the object is represented by a dot (or circle), and the forces acting on it are represented by arrows originating from that dot.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0008-10.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0008-10.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0008-10.png]**
> The graphic is a cartoon illustration featuring a hot-air balloon shaped like planet Earth, with two figures in the basket holding telescopes, surrounded by clouds, birds, the sun, and a thought bubble containing the text "VISIT Scientists discover star devouring nearby planet. bit.ly/1aDCvCz". Visible elements include a globe-patterned balloon envelope, a wicker basket, a burner flame, and observer figures with handheld telescopes. Conceptually, this visual serves as an engaging stimulus for high school learners exploring space science and astronomy, encouraging inquiry-based learning regarding planetary systems and stellar phenomena.



To calculate the **net force** acting on Rod:
$$\text{Net force} = 10\text{ N} + (-6\text{ N}) = 4\text{ N to the left}$$

---

### Summary Rule
If multiple forces act on an object in the **same direction**, the net force is the sum of the individual forces:
$$\text{Net force} = F_{1} + F_{2} + ... + F_{n}$$

# Forces

## Net Force
When two or more forces act on an object in the same direction, they are added together to produce a **net force**. The net force is the sum of the individual forces.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0009-03.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0009-03.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0009-03.png]**
> This educational graphic is a comparative scale representation illustrating stellar sizes in astronomy. It features the massive red hypergiant star labeled "VY Canis Majoris" alongside a magnified inset showing a tiny dot labeled "Sun" and a dashed curve labeled "Earth's orbit". Conceptually, this diagram helps high school learners comprehend the immense scale and diversity of stellar objects in the universe by visually contrasting our Sun with one of the largest known stars.



**Example Calculation:**
If Force 1 ($F_1$) is $7\text{ N}$ and Force 2 ($F_2$) is $10\text{ N}$ acting in the same direction:
$$\text{Net Force} = F_1 + F_2$$
$$\text{Net Force} = 7\text{ N} + 10\text{ N} = 17\text{ N}$$

---

## Measuring Force
Forces are measured using an instrument called a **spring balance**.

*   **Definition:** A spring balance is a device containing a spring that stretches when a force is applied to it. The distance the spring stretches provides a reading of the force applied.
*   **Did You Know? (Hooke's Law):** The functioning of a spring balance is based on Hooke's Law, which states that the distance a spring moves from its rest position is directly proportional to the size of the force applied.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0009-04.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0009-04.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0009-04.png]**
> The graphic is an environmental education illustration featuring a globe surrounded by a continuous ring of green recycling arrows and a figure holding a blank sign. Visible elements include a planet Earth graphic, a cartoon human figure in green clothing holding a vertical post attached to a blank banner, and multiple curving green arrows arranged in a circular recycling flow pattern. Conceptually, the diagram conveys the interconnectedness of human action and global sustainability, introducing learners to the core CAPS concepts of environmental responsibility, resource management, and recycling within Earth's ecosystems.



---

## Types of Forces
Forces can be categorized by whether they require physical contact between objects. While some forces require contact, others can act over a distance.

### Types of Forces

Forces can be exerted without objects being in direct contact. There are two broad categories of forces:

*   **Contact forces:** Objects are in physical contact with each other and exert forces on each other.
*   **Non-contact (field) forces:** Objects are not in physical contact with each other but still exert forces on each other.

---

### 1.2 Contact Forces

Contact forces occur between objects that are touching each other. Examples include pushing a desk or pulling a go-cart.

#### Friction
Friction is a force that opposes motion. When an object moves across a surface, friction acts in the opposite direction to the motion of the object.

*   **Example:** When a ball is kicked across grass, it moves quickly at first but eventually slows down and stops. This happens because friction acts backwards on the ball, resisting its forward movement.

**New Words:**
*   Friction
*   Normal force
*   Tension
*   Compression

### Forces Acting on Objects

#### Understanding Friction
Friction is a force that acts in the opposite direction to the motion of an object. When an object moves across a surface, friction works to resist that motion.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0011-01.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0011-01.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0011-01.png]**
> The graphic is a comparative scale representation displaying planetary and stellar objects. Visible labels include "Uranus", "Neptune", "Earth", "Sirius B", and "Venus" positioned next to their respective spherical bodies. This comparative visual conveys the vast differences in physical size between gas giants like Uranus and Neptune and terrestrial or stellar bodies such as Earth, Venus, and Sirius B.



> **TAKE NOTE**
> There are other forces acting on a system in motion, such as friction and weight.

#### Factors Affecting Force
To determine the amount of force required to move an object, we must consider all the forces acting upon it. 

Consider the example of two learners pushing a go-cart:
*   **Applied Forces:** $F_1 = 7\text{ N}$ and $F_2 = 10\text{ N}$ acting in the direction of motion.
*   **Total Applied Force:** The sum of the forces is $F_{total} = F_1 + F_2 = 7\text{ N} + 10\text{ N} = 17\text{ N}$.

#### Identifying Forces in a System
When an object like a go-cart moves, it is subject to multiple forces:
1.  **Applied Force:** The push from the learners.
2.  **Friction:** Acts in the opposite direction to the motion of the go-cart.
3.  **Weight:** The gravitational force of the Earth acting on the object.

#### Free-Body Diagrams
A free-body diagram represents all forces acting on an object as vectors originating from a single point.

Conceptually, this conveys how passing an electric current through a solenoid produces a magnetic field strong enough to magnetize and attract metal objects, demonstrating the principles of electromagnetism for CAPS learners.

### The Normal Force

The **normal force** ($F_N$) is a contact force that prevents an object from penetrating the surface it is resting on. 

*   **Direction:** The normal force always acts perpendicularly (at a $90^\circ$ angle) to the surface that the object is resting on.
*   **Relationship to Weight:** In simple situations, such as an object resting on a flat, level surface, the normal force is equal in magnitude to the weight of the object ($F_w$), but acts in the opposite direction.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0012-00.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0012-00.png)


### Free-Body Diagram of a Go-Cart
When representing the forces acting on a go-cart on a level surface, we include the normal force acting upwards, the weight acting downwards, and the horizontal forces (friction and applied forces $F_1$ and $F_2$).

### Investigation: The Relationship Between Normal Force and Friction

**Investigative Question:** What is the relationship between the normal force and the frictional force?

**Aim:** To determine the relationship between the normal force and the size of the frictional force.

**Methodology:**
1.  Place an object on a flat, level surface.
2.  Increase the mass of the object incrementally.
3.  Measure the resulting frictional force for each mass.
4.  Analyze how the increase in mass (which increases the weight and therefore the normal force) affects the frictional force.

**Key Principle:**
On a flat, level surface, the normal force is equal to the weight of the object. By increasing the mass, you increase the weight, which in turn increases the normal force exerted by the surface on the object.

### Investigation: Measuring Static Friction

#### Key Concepts
*   **Weight ($W$):** Calculated using the formula $W = m \times g$, where $m$ is the mass in $\text{kg}$ and $g$ is the gravitational acceleration ($9,8 \text{ m/s}^2$).
*   **Static Friction:** The frictional force between two objects that are not moving. It is measured as the minimum force required to start the block moving.
*   **Reliability:** Taking three readings and calculating an average increases the reliability of experimental results.

#### Materials and Apparatus
*   Wooden blocks with different known masses or mass pieces.
*   Wooden block with a hook.
*   Spring balance.
*   Triple beam balance or electronic scale.

#### Method
1.  Measure the mass of the wooden block with a hook using the balance. Record the mass and calculate the normal force.
2.  Place the wooden block on the table and attach the spring balance to the hook. Mark a starting position on the desk.
3.  Pull the block sideways until it just starts to move.
4.  Record the force reading in the table. Repeat this process three times for the same block.
5.  Add a mass piece to the top of the wooden block. Calculate the new total mass and the corresponding normal force. Repeat the pulling process three times.
6.  Repeat the experiment for various larger masses and complete the results table.

The force applied to the point at which the block starts to move is equal to the frictional force acting on the block.

### Investigation: Factors Affecting Friction

#### Analysis Questions
6. What do you think would happen if the block was not placed on the smooth desk, but rather on a rough surface, or a much smoother surface? Will this affect the results?

---

### Conclusion
1. Write a conclusion for this investigation.
2. Repeat the investigation and take some readings from the spring balance once the block is moving. How do the readings for the stationary block and a moving block compare? Is there a difference?

---

### Key Concepts: Friction and Net Force

*   **Definition:** The force of friction depends on the type of surface on which an object is moving and the normal force.
*   **Overcoming Friction:** To move an object, an applied force must be greater than the frictional force acting between the object and the surface.
*   **Net Force Calculation:** When multiple forces act on an object, the net force is the sum of forces in the direction of motion minus the opposing frictional force.

#### Worked Example: Go-Cart Forces
Consider a go-cart being pushed forward by two forces of $7\text{ N}$ and $10\text{ N}$, with a frictional force of $5\text{ N}$ acting in the opposite direction.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0016-00.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0016-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0016-00.png]**
> This graphic is a telescopic astronomical image showing an expanding nebula with intricate filamentary gas structures and a glowing central region against a star-filled dark background, with no explicit text labels or symbols present. Aligned with the CAPS Physical Sciences curriculum, this visual illustrates the end stages of massive stellar evolution and supernova remnants, helping learners understand how heavier elements are dispersed into the interstellar medium.



**Calculation of Net Force:**
The net force ($F_{net}$) is calculated as:
$$F_{net} = (\text{Forward Forces}) - (\text{Frictional Force})$$
$$F_{net} = (7\text{ N} + 10\text{ N}) - 5\text{ N}$$
$$F_{net} = 17\text{ N} - 5\text{ N}$$
$$F_{net} = 12\text{ N}$$

---

### Did You Know?
*   **Heat Generation:** Friction between two surfaces causes objects to heat up. You can test this by rubbing your hands together vigorously.
*   **Practical Applications:** Friction is advantageous as it allows us to walk without slipping and prevents cars from skidding by providing grip between the tyres and the road.

---

*   **Visit:** For more information on how friction causes materials to heat up and melt, visit: `bit.ly/190eYdz`

# Tension and Compression

Tension and compression are types of **contact forces**. A contact force is a force that acts on an object only when it is in physical contact with another object.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0017-01.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0017-01.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0017-01.png]**
> The graphic displays a simple, cloud-shaped text box containing the capitalized word "VISIT". No additional labels, symbols, or arrows are present within the image. Conceptually, this visual acts as a generic call-to-action banner or prompt rather than conveying a specific academic or subject-matter concept aligned with the CAPS curriculum.



## 1. Tension
Tension is a pulling force transmitted through a string, rope, cable, or wire when it is pulled tight by forces acting from opposite ends.

*   **Example:** When a person pulls a block with a rope, the person pulls the rope, and the rope pulls the block. 
*   **Mechanism:** Because the rope is pulled in opposite directions, it becomes tight. This state of being "tight" is what we define as tension. The tension in the rope is the force that allows the block to be moved across the floor.

## 2. Compression
Compression is a pushing force that acts to deform, squash, or squeeze an object.

*   **Definition:** A compression force acts to reduce the volume of an object or change its shape by pressing it inward.
*   **Examples:**
    *   **Dough:** When you crush a ball of dough with your fingers, you exert a compression force, causing the dough to deform and change shape.
    *   **Tennis Ball:** Squeezing a tennis ball between the palms of your hands.
    *   **Cooldrink Can:** Crushing a can between your palms.

### Tension and Compression

Forces can act on objects to change their shape or state of motion. Two primary types of contact forces are tension and compression.

*   **Tension:** Two forces acting on one object in opposite directions (away from each other) to stretch the object.
*   **Compression:** Two forces acting on one object in opposite directions (towards each other) to compress or deform the object.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Cost-of-electrical-energy.pdf-0018-07.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Cost-of-electrical-energy.pdf-0018-07.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Cost-of-electrical-energy.pdf-0018-07.png]**
> 1. A green and white diagram of a spring and battery.
 2. A green and white diagram of a spring and battery.
 3. A green and white diagram of a spring and battery.



#### Real-world Application: Bridges
Bridges are common examples of structures that experience both tension and compression simultaneously. When a load (such as a vehicle) is placed on a bridge, the weight creates a downward force. This causes the top of the bridge structure to experience **compression** (being pushed together) while the bottom of the structure experiences **tension** (being pulled apart).

The globe is adorned with a map of the world, and the balloon is carrying two people on its basket. The scene is set against a backdrop of clouds and a blue sky. The diagram is a simple, yet effective illustration of the Earth's geography and the concept of a hot air

### 1.3 Field (Non-contact) Forces

While contact forces (like friction, normal force, tension, and compression) require physical interaction, there is another category of forces that act between bodies that do not touch.

#### Definition: Field Force
A **field** is a region in space where an object (possessing certain properties) will experience a force. Field forces are **non-contact forces** because they act over a distance without the objects needing to be in physical contact.

#### New Words
*   **Gravitational force:** A force of attraction between objects with mass.
*   **Magnetic force:** A force of attraction or repulsion between magnetic materials.
*   **Electrostatic force:** A force between charged objects.
*   **Field forces:** Forces that act over a distance.
*   **Attraction:** A force that pulls objects together.
*   **Repulsion:** A force that pushes objects apart.

# Chapter 1: Forces

## Types of Fields
Fields are regions in space where an object experiences a force. The most common examples of fields are:
*   **Gravitational field**
*   **Magnetic field**
*   **Electric field**

While contact forces are described as "pushes" and "pulls," field forces are better described in terms of **repulsion** and **attraction**.

---

## Gravitational Forces

### Definition
The **gravitational force** is the force that causes objects with mass to be attracted to one another. It is the force that pulls objects towards the Earth and prevents us from drifting off into space.

### Key Principles
*   **Universal Attraction:** Any object with mass exerts a gravitational force on any other object with mass.
*   **Direction:** The Earth's gravitational force pulls everything towards the centre of the Earth.
*   **Reciprocity:** Gravitational forces are mutual. If the Earth pulls on you, you exert an equal but opposite pull on the Earth.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0019-10.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0019-10.png)


### Critical Thinking Question
*Why do you think these forces on the Earth do not cause the Earth to move noticeably?*

---

## Did You Know?
Isaac Newton developed the **Law of Universal Gravitation** in 1687. His work on describing the force of attraction between bodies with mass was famously inspired by observing an apple falling from a tree.

---

## Take Note
*   **Measurement:** Gravity is a force and is therefore measured in **Newtons (N)**.

---

*   **Visit:** For more information on gravity, visit: [bit.ly/1gN57fo](http://bit.ly/1gN57fo)

# Gravity and the Solar System

## The Concept of Gravity
Gravity is a force of attraction that exists between all objects that have mass. The Earth has a very large mass, which is why it exerts a constant downward pull on us, keeping us attracted towards its centre.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0020-00.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0020-00.png)


## Factors Affecting Gravitational Force
The strength of the gravitational force between two objects is determined by their mass:
*   **Mass and Force:** The greater the mass of the objects, the stronger the gravitational force between them.
*   **Small Objects:** Two small objects have a very weak gravitational attraction, which often has no noticeable effect.
*   **Large Objects:** Massive objects, such as the Moon and the Earth, exert a much greater gravitational force on each other.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0020-02.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0020-02.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0020-02.png]**
> This educational graphic illustrates the stellar evolution lifecycle of an average-sized star against a deep space background, labeled sequentially with letters A through E and connected by directional arrows. The visible stages depict a nebula (A), a main-sequence star like our Sun (B), a red giant (C), a planetary nebula with a white dwarf core (D), and a final fading white dwarf (E). Conceptually, this diagram helps high school learners understand the chronological stages of stellar life cycles, demonstrating how stars change in size, colour, and state over cosmic time.



## Gravity in the Solar System
In our solar system, gravity acts as the "glue" that keeps the system together. 

*   **Orbits:** All planets in our solar system are held in orbit around the Sun due to the gravitational force of attraction between the Sun and the planets.
*   **Interplanetary Attraction:** There is a gravitational force of attraction not only between the Sun and the planets but also between planets and their respective moons.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0020-10.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0020-10.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0020-10.png]**
> This horizontal infographic illustrates animal behavior, specifically an ant trail consisting of numerous individual ants following a chemical path. The graphic depicts a single prominent ant alongside long, linear streams of tiny ants moving along a shaded pathway. Conceptually, it conveys the mechanism of social communication and pheromone tracking in insects, demonstrating how collective animal behaviour is coordinated in Life Sciences.

### Gravitational Force: Key Concepts

The gravitational force of attraction between two objects is determined by two primary factors:

1.  **Mass:** The greater the mass of the objects, the stronger the gravitational force of attraction between them.
2.  **Distance:** The closer objects are to each other, the stronger the gravitational force of attraction between them. Conversely, the further objects are away from each other, the smaller the gravitational force.

---

### Investigation: Dropping Objects

**Investigative Question:** Do different objects fall at the same rate?

**Hypothesis:** [Record your prediction here regarding whether mass or volume affects the rate at which objects fall.]



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Cost-of-electrical-energy.pdf-0021-04.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Cost-of-electrical-energy.pdf-0021-04.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Cost-of-electrical-energy.pdf-0021-04.png]**
> 1. A diagram of a cell with arrows pointing to different parts of the cell.



#### Materials and Apparatus
*   Hammer
*   Feather
*   Two balls of the same mass, different volumes (one set per pair)
*   Two balls of the same volume, different masses (one set per pair)

#### Method
1.  Work in pairs: one person acts as the **experimenter** (drops the object) and the other as the **observer**.
2.  Complete the prediction column in the table below before performing the drops.
3.  **Experimenter:** Stand on a chair or desk. Hold the two balls of the same mass in each hand.
4.  **Experimenter:** Hold the balls at the same height and drop them at exactly the same time.
5.  **Observer:** Note which ball lands first.
6.  Swap positions and repeat the experiment using the two balls of the same volume but different masses.
7.  **Teacher Demonstration:** The teacher will drop a hammer and a feather. Record your prediction before the drop, then record the actual result.

#### Observation Table

| Object Pair | Prediction (Which lands first?) | Result (Which landed first?) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Same mass, different volume | | |
| Same volume, different mass | | |
| Hammer and feather | | |

### Did You Know?
There is a gravitational force of attraction between us and the Sun. We do not notice this force because we are very far apart, and our mass is very small compared to the Sun.

### Chapter 1: Forces

#### New Vocabulary
*   **Weight:** The gravitational force of attraction exerted on an object by a planet (e.g., Earth).
*   **Mass:** The amount of matter in an object.
*   **Free-fall:** The motion of an object falling solely under the influence of gravity.
*   **Gravitational acceleration:** The rate at which an object speeds up as it falls due to gravity.

---

#### Discussion Questions
2. Considering the balls of the same mass, which landed first, the larger one or the smaller one?
3. Considering the balls of the same volume, which landed first, the heavier one or the lighter one?
4. Why do you think the two dropped balls always landed at the same time?
5. Why do you think the hammer landed before the feather?

---

#### Concept: Mass vs. Weight

**Common Misconception:**
In everyday language, people often use the term "weight" incorrectly. For example, saying "My weight increased by 2 kg" is scientifically inaccurate because kilograms are a unit of mass, not weight.

**Definitions:**

| Concept | Definition | Unit | Dependency |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Mass** | The amount of matter in an object (number of atoms/particles). | Kilograms (kg) | Independent of location. |
| **Weight** | The gravitational force of attraction exerted on an object. | Newtons (N) | Depends on the mass of the object and the strength of gravity at that location. |

**Key Takeaways:**
*   **Mass:** A wooden block with a mass of $10\text{ kg}$ on Earth will still have a mass of $10\text{ kg}$ on the Moon.
*   **Weight:** Because weight is a force of attraction, it changes depending on where the object is weighed (e.g., Earth vs. the Moon).

Conceptually, the diagram conveys to high school learners that hotter stars emit shorter wavelengths appearing blue-white, while cooler stars emit longer wavelengths appearing red, supporting the study of electromagnetic radiation and stellar characteristics in Earth and Space Sciences.

The weight of a $10\text{ kg}$ block on Earth is different from its weight on the Moon.

### Investigation: The Relationship Between Mass and Weight

**Investigative Question:** What is the relationship between the mass of an object and its weight?

**Hypothesis:** [Space provided for learner to formulate a prediction]

#### Materials and Apparatus
*   Four mass pieces in increments of $500\text{ g}$ ($500\text{ g}$, $1\text{ kg}$, $1,5\text{ kg}$, and $2\text{ kg}$).
*   Spring balance.
*   Triple beam balance.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0024-10.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0024-10.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Birth-life-and-the-death-of-a-star.pdf-0024-10.png]**

The diagram features directional arrows connecting successive stages labeled as "stellar nebula", "main sequence", "yellow star same size as our Sun", "dying star", "red giant", "planetary nebula", and "white dwarf". Conceptually, it conveys the life cycle and stellar evolution of average-mass stars from initial cloud collapse to its final remnant state for Grade 10-12 Physical Sciences learners studying Earth and Beyond.



#### Method
1. Measure the mass pieces using the triple beam balance.
2. Measure the weight of each mass piece using the spring balance.
3. Record the mass and matching weight in the results table.
4. Draw a graph of your results.
5. Calculate the gradient of the graph.

### Chapter 1: Forces - Data Analysis and Graphing

#### 1. Conceptual Definitions
*   **Independent Variable:** The variable that is changed or controlled in a scientific experiment to test the effects on the dependent variable. It is typically plotted on the $x$-axis (horizontal axis).
*   **Dependent Variable:** The variable being tested and measured in a scientific experiment. It changes based on the independent variable. It is typically plotted on the $y$-axis (vertical axis).
*   **Gradient ($m$):** The measure of the steepness and direction of a line. For a straight-line graph, it is calculated as the change in the dependent variable divided by the change in the independent variable.

#### 2. Exercises

**Question 1**
Identify the dependent variable from your experimental data.

**Question 2**
Identify the independent variable from your experimental data.

**Question 3: Graphing**
Plot your data points on the grid provided below. Ensure you label your axes and include units.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Cost-of-electrical-energy.pdf-0025-00.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Cost-of-electrical-energy.pdf-0025-00.png)


**Question 4: Gradient Calculation**
Calculate the gradient of your straight-line graph using the formula:

$$m = \frac{\Delta y}{\Delta x} = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}$$

Use the space below for your calculation:

| Calculation Step | Formula/Value |
| :--- | :--- |
| Select two points on the line | $(x_1, y_1)$ and $(x_2, y_2)$ |
| Change in $y$ ($\Delta y$) | $y_2 - y_1$ |
| Change in $x$ ($\Delta x$) | $x_2 - x_1$ |
| Final Gradient ($m$) | $\frac{\Delta y}{\Delta x}$ |

### Conclusion
Write a conclusion for this investigation.

---

### Key Concepts: Weight and Gravity

**Weight** is defined as the force of gravity pulling an object towards the centre of the Earth. It is measured in **newtons (N)**.

*   **Gravitational Acceleration ($g$):** On Earth, the gravitational force causes objects to accelerate towards the centre of the Earth at a rate of $9,8 \text{ m/s}^2$.
*   **Free-fall:** An object is in free-fall when the only force acting upon it is the gravitational force.
*   **Calculating Weight:** Weight ($W$) is calculated by multiplying an object's mass ($m$) by the gravitational acceleration ($g$).

$$W = m \times g$$



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Cost-of-electrical-energy.pdf-0026-00.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Cost-of-electrical-energy.pdf-0026-00.png)


> **TAKE NOTE**
> The formula $W = m \times g$ is also used in calculations involving friction to determine the normal force acting on an object.

---

### Gravity on Other Celestial Bodies

The strength of gravity varies depending on the mass of the planet or moon. While your weight changes depending on where you are, your **mass remains constant**.

*   **The Moon:** The gravity on the surface of the Moon is $\frac{1}{6}$ of the gravity on Earth. You would weigh one-sixth of your Earth weight on the Moon.
*   **Jupiter:** The gravity on Jupiter is $2,5$ times that of Earth. You would weigh $2,5$ times more on Jupiter than you do on Earth.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Cost-of-electrical-energy.pdf-0026-02.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Cost-of-electrical-energy.pdf-0026-02.png)


> **DID YOU KNOW?**
> The mass of the Earth is $5,972 \times 10^{24} \text{ kg}$.

### Mass vs. Weight

#### Key Concepts
*   **Mass:** The amount of matter in an object. An object's mass remains constant regardless of its location in the universe.
*   **Weight:** The force exerted on an object due to gravity. Weight depends on the gravitational acceleration of the location where it is measured.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Energy-and-the-national-electricity-grid.pdf-0027-01.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Energy-and-the-national-electricity-grid.pdf-0027-01.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Energy-and-the-national-electricity-grid.pdf-0027-01.png]**
> This educational graphic is an overview diagram illustrating the generation and transmission of electrical energy from a power station to consumers. Visible labels include "power station", "coal", "transformer", "national transmission lines", "substation", and "houses, shops and other buildings", along with sectional reference letters A, B, C, and D. For a CAPS Physical Sciences or Technology learner, this diagram conveys the macro-level path of electricity generation and distribution, emphasizing the role of step-up and step-down transformers in minimizing energy loss over long-distance transmission lines.



#### Take Note
A bathroom scale actually measures weight (the force exerted on the scale) and converts it to a mass reading.

#### Calculating Weight
Weight is calculated using the formula:
$$W = m \times g$$

Where:
*   $W$ = Weight (measured in Newtons, $N$)
*   $m$ = Mass (measured in kilograms, $kg$)
*   $g$ = Gravitational acceleration (on Earth, $g \approx 9,8 \, m/s^2$)

**Example:**
If you have a mass of $60 \, kg$:
*   **On Earth:** $60 \, kg \times 9,8 \, m/s^2 = 588 \, N$
*   **On the Moon:** $60 \, kg \times 1,6 \, m/s^2 = 96 \, N$

### ACTIVITY: Weight and mass calculations

**Question 1:**
A Ferrari has a mass of $1485 \, kg$. What is its weight on Earth?



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0027-00.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0027-00.png)


**Solution:**
To calculate the weight on Earth, use the gravitational acceleration constant $g = 9,8 \, m/s^2$:

$$W = 1485 \, kg \times 9,8 \, m/s^2$$
$$W = 14553 \, N$$

### Exercises: Mass and Weight

**2. Lindiwe has a mass of 50 kg on Earth. What is her mass on the Moon?**

**3. Ian has a mass of 78 kg. His friend Sam says that he would weigh 24 N on the Moon. Is Sam correct? Explain by using a calculation.**

*   **Key Concept:** Weight ($W$) is calculated using the formula $W = m \times g$, where $m$ is mass in kilograms (kg) and $g$ is the gravitational acceleration.
*   **Note:** The gravitational acceleration on the Moon is approximately $g_{moon} \approx 1,6 \, \text{m/s}^2$.

**4. You have an apple with a mass of 220 g, what is its weight on Earth and on the Moon?**

*   **Conversion:** First, convert the mass to SI units: $220 \, \text{g} = 0,22 \, \text{kg}$.
*   **Earth Calculation:** Use $g_{earth} \approx 9,8 \, \text{m/s}^2$.
*   **Moon Calculation:** Use $g_{moon} \approx 1,6 \, \text{m/s}^2$.

**5. If a cow weighed 1340 N on the Moon, what is its mass?**

*   **Formula Rearrangement:** To find mass ($m$) when weight ($W$) and gravity ($g$) are known, use:
    $$m = \frac{W}{g}$$

**Summary of Constants for Calculations:**
*   **Earth Gravity ($g$):** $9,8 \, \text{m/s}^2$
*   **Moon Gravity ($g$):** $1,6 \, \text{m/s}^2$

*Note: Mass is the amount of matter in an object and remains constant regardless of location. Weight is the force of gravity acting on that mass and changes depending on the gravitational field strength of the location.*

# Activity: How much would you weigh on other planets?



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0029-00.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0029-00.png)


### Materials
*   Weighing scales
*   Calculator

### Instructions
1.  Measure your mass in kilograms ($kg$). Record this value in the "Your mass (kg)" column for every planet in the table below.
2.  Use the values for the acceleration due to gravity ($g$) on various planets to calculate your weight ($W$) on that planet using the formula:
    $$W = m \times g$$
    Where:
    *   $W$ = Weight in Newtons ($N$)
    *   $m$ = Mass in kilograms ($kg$)
    *   $g$ = Acceleration due to gravity in meters per second squared ($m/s^2$)

### Data Table

| Planet | Your mass (kg) | Value of $g$ ($m/s^2$) | Your weight (N) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Earth | | $9,8$ | |
| Mercury | | $3,6$ | |
| Venus | | $8,8$ | |
| Mars | | $3,8$ | |
| Jupiter | | $26$ | |
| Saturn | | $11,2$ | |
| Uranus | | $10,5$ | |
| Neptune | | $13,3$ | |

### Questions
1. On which planets would you feel heavier than you do on Earth?
2. On which planets would you feel lighter than you do on Earth?

### Weight and Weightlessness

*   **Definition of Weight:** The weight of a person is the force of gravitational attraction to the Earth that the person experiences.
*   **Weightlessness:** A person in free-fall feels weightless, but they have not lost their weight; they are still experiencing the Earth's gravitational attraction.
*   **Astronauts in Space:** Astronauts appear to float because both the astronauts and their spacecraft are in free-fall, falling toward the Earth at the same rate.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0030-07.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0030-07.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0030-07.png]**
> 1. A diagram of a roller coaster with a spring coiled up on top.



---

### Magnetic Forces

*   **Magnets:** Certain materials have strong magnetic fields around them. These are called magnets.
*   **Poles:** All magnets have two poles: a north pole and a south pole.
*   **Magnetic Materials:** Materials that are strongly attracted to magnets are called magnetic materials. Magnets exert forces on other magnets and on magnetic materials.

**New Words:**
*   **Magnet:** An object that produces a magnetic field.
*   **Magnetic material:** A material that is attracted to a magnet.
*   **Alloy:** A metal made by combining two or more metallic elements.

### Key Concepts and Notes

*   **Magnetic Materials:** Not all metals are attracted to magnets. Only specific materials are classified as magnetic.
*   **Common Magnetic Materials:** The most common magnetic materials are **iron**, **nickel**, and **cobalt**.
*   **Alloys:** An alloy is a mixture of metals. 
    *   *Note:* Alloys that include magnetic materials can also be attracted to magnets.
    *   *Example:* Steel is an alloy that contains iron; therefore, steel is attracted to a magnet.
*   **Magnetic Force:** Magnetic forces are non-contact forces, meaning they can act over a distance without the objects touching.

# Lesson: Investigating Magnetic Forces

## Core Concept
Magnetic force is a non-contact force that can act through space and various materials. This activity explores how magnetic poles interact and whether magnetic fields can penetrate different substances.

---

## Activity: Can a magnetic force act through substances?

### Materials Required
*   Bar magnets
*   Paper
*   Thin piece of wood
*   Thick piece of wood
*   Foil
*   Paperclips

### Experimental Procedure

| Step | Action | Observation Goal |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 1 | Hold two north poles close together. | Observe the interaction (repulsion). |
| 2 | Hold two south poles close together. | Observe the interaction (repulsion). |
| 3 | Hold a north pole and a south pole close together. | Observe the interaction (attraction). |
| 4 | Place paperclips on the desk. | Establish a baseline. |
| 5 | Attempt to pick up paperclips with a magnet, placing a material between them. | Determine if the force is blocked. |
| 6 | Repeat step 5 for all provided materials. | Compare results across different substances. |

---

## Analysis and Questions

### 1. Material Interference
Observe whether the magnetic force is obstructed by the materials tested. 
*   *Note:* Magnetic fields generally pass through non-magnetic materials (like paper, wood, and foil) without significant reduction in force, provided the distance is not too great.

### 2. Nature of Magnetic Force
Based on your observations, consider the following:
*   **Interaction:** Like poles repel, while opposite poles attract.
*   **Transmission:** Magnetic fields are not blocked by most common non-magnetic materials. The force is a field that exists in the space surrounding the magnet, allowing it to exert influence even when a physical barrier is present.

---

## Further Exploration
To deepen your understanding of the invisible region where these forces operate, investigate the concept of the **magnetic field**. 

*   **Definition:** A magnetic field is a vector field that describes the magnetic influence on moving electric charges, electric currents, and magnetic materials.
*   **Representation:** We often visualize these fields using field lines, where the density of the lines indicates the strength of the field:
    $$B = \frac{F}{qv \sin(\theta)}$$
    *(Where $B$ is the magnetic field strength, $F$ is the force, $q$ is the charge, $v$ is the velocity, and $\theta$ is the angle between velocity and the field.)*

### Magnetic Forces and Fields

#### Key Concepts
*   **Magnetic Poles:** Like poles repel each other, while opposite poles attract each other.
*   **Non-contact Force:** A magnetic force acts over a distance. A magnet does not need to physically touch an object to exert a force on it.
*   **Magnetic Field:** The region of space around a magnet where a magnetic force can be detected.

# Magnetic Force Fields

## 1. Concept: The Magnetic Field
A magnetic field is the region of space surrounding a magnet within which the magnet can exert an attractive or repulsive force on another magnet or magnetic material. Magnetic force acts over a distance.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0035-01.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0035-01.png)


> **TAKE NOTE**
> While iron filings demonstrate a two-dimensional view of a magnetic field, the field actually exists in three dimensions all around the magnet.

## 2. Representing Magnetic Fields: Field Lines
Because magnetic fields are invisible, we use **field lines** to represent them visually. These lines follow specific rules:

*   **Direction:** Field lines always travel from the **North pole** to the **South pole**.
*   **Strength:** The density of the lines indicates the strength of the field.
    *   The closer the lines are drawn together, the **stronger** the magnetic field.
    *   The more field lines drawn in a specific area, the **stronger** the field.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0035-02.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0035-02.png)


## 3. Interaction Between Magnets
Magnetic field lines change shape depending on how magnets are positioned relative to one another.

### Opposite Poles Attracting
When opposite poles (North and South) are placed near each other, the field lines connect the two magnets, pulling them together.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0035-08.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0035-08.png)

# Magnetism and the Earth

## 1. Magnetic Field Properties
*   **Interaction:** Like magnetic poles repel each other.
*   **Field Strength:** A magnetic field is strongest in the immediate vicinity of the magnet and decreases in strength as the distance from the magnet increases.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0036-01.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0036-01.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0036-01.png]**
> 1. A black and white diagram of a molecule with two hydrogen atoms and one oxygen atom.



## 2. The Earth as a Magnet
The Earth acts like a giant bar magnet with its own North and South magnetic poles. 

*   **The Theory:** Scientists believe that the Earth's magnetic field is generated by the movement of superhot liquid iron within the Earth's core. This rotational movement creates weak magnetic forces around the Earth's rotational axis.
*   **Orientation:** The Earth's magnetic field is oriented such that the "South Pole" of this internal bar magnet is located under the Earth's geographic North Pole.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0036-03.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0036-03.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0036-03.png]**

The boat is depicted in a cartoon style, with a blue background and white text. The text on the image provides a brief description of the scene, including the number of people on the boat, the



## 3. Compasses and Navigation
The Earth's magnetic field allows us to use compasses for navigation.

*   **How a Compass Works:** A plotting compass contains a small, magnetized needle. 
*   **Principle of Attraction:** Because the needle is a small magnet, it is attracted to the opposite magnetic pole of the Earth. The needle aligns itself with the Earth's magnetic field, pointing toward magnetic north, which allows users to determine direction.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0036-05.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0036-05.png)

## 4. Key Terminology
*   **Aurora Australis:** The "Southern Lights," a natural light display in the Earth's sky, predominantly seen in high-latitude regions around the Arctic and Antarctic.
*   **Aurora Borealis:** The "Northern Lights," a similar natural light display occurring in the Northern Hemisphere.

# Natural Sciences: Magnetic and Electrostatic Forces

## 1. Magnetic Phenomena in Nature
The Earth is surrounded by a magnetic field that interacts with charged particles from the Sun.

*   **The Aurora (Southern and Northern Lights):**
    *   Charged particles are emitted from the Sun's surface and travel through space.
    *   When these particles reach Earth, they are trapped by the Earth's magnetic field in regions known as "belts."
    *   Particles occasionally escape these belts and spiral along magnetic field lines toward the magnetic poles.
    *   As they enter the atmosphere, they interact with atmospheric gas particles, resulting in the visible light displays known as the Southern or Northern Lights.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0037-01.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0037-01.png)

The man is pointing to the globe, which is positioned in the center of the image. The image also includes a speech bubble containing text that reads "Visit Earth's magnetic field."



*   **Ferrofluids:**
    *   **Definition:** A ferrofluid is a liquid that becomes strongly magnetised in the presence of a magnetic field.

### Investigation: Static Electricity

#### Experimental Procedure

1. **Balloon and Hair:**
   * Rub your hair with the balloon.
   * Hold the balloon a short distance away from your hair. Observe the interaction.

2. **Glass Rod and Paper:**
   * Hold the glass rod over small pieces of paper. Observe the interaction.
   * Rub the glass rod with the knitted fabric.
   * Hold the glass rod over the pieces of paper again. Observe the interaction.

3. **Glass Rod and Water:**
   * Rub the glass rod with the knitted fabric.
   * Open the tap to create a thin, steady stream of water.
   * Hold the glass rod close to the stream of water. Observe the interaction.

---

#### Questions

1. What action was taken to make your hair stick to the balloon?
2. What happens when you rub the glass rod with the knitted fabric?
3. Why did the glass rod attract the stream of water?

---

### Concept: Friction and Electron Transfer

When two surfaces are rubbed together, **friction** is created between them. This friction can cause electrons to be transferred from one surface to the other.

#### Understanding Atomic Structure
To understand how electrons are transferred, we must recall the structure of an atom:

*   **Protons:** Positively charged particles located in the nucleus.
*   **Neutrons:** Neutral particles located in the nucleus.
*   **Electrons:** Negatively charged particles that orbit the nucleus.

**Key Question:** Where are the electrons positioned in the atom?

### Atomic Structure and Electrostatics

#### Key Concepts
*   **Proton:** A subatomic particle with a positive charge.
*   **Electron:** A subatomic particle with a negative charge.
*   **Neutron:** A subatomic particle with no charge (neutral).
*   **Electrostatic Attraction:** The force that holds an atom together, acting between the positively charged nucleus and the negatively charged electrons.
*   **Neutrality:** Atoms are normally neutral because they contain an equal number of protons and electrons, balancing the positive and negative charges.

#### The Transfer of Electrons
While protons are fixed in the nucleus and cannot move between atoms, electrons can be transferred from one object to another through friction (e.g., rubbing a balloon against hair or combing hair).

*   **Rule of Transfer:** Only electrons move. Protons remain stationary within the nucleus.
*   **Result of Transfer:** When an object loses electrons, it becomes positively charged. When an object gains electrons, it becomes negatively charged.

---

#### Exercises

**1. Concept Check**
*   What is the type of charge on a proton?
*   What is the type of charge on an electron?
*   What is the charge on a neutron?

**2. Diagram Analysis**


![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0039-00.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0039-00.png)


*   Based on the provided diagram, which object gave up some of its electrons?
*   Does this object now have more positive or more negative charges?

---

#### Take Note
*   **Movement:** Only the outer electrons move.
*   **Fixed Position:** Protons are located in the nucleus and do not move between atoms.

### Understanding Electric Charge

#### Key Concepts
*   **Negatively charged:** An object that has more electrons than protons.
*   **Positively charged:** An object that has fewer electrons than protons.
*   **Neutral:** An object where the number of positive charges equals the number of negative charges, resulting in a net charge of zero.

#### Charge Calculations
The overall charge of an object is determined by the sum of its positive and negative charges.

| Object State | Calculation | Overall Charge |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Neutral | $6 + (-6) = 0$ | $0$ |
| Positively Charged | $8 + (-6) = 2$ | $+2$ |
| Negatively Charged | $6 + (-9) = -3$ | $-3$ |

### Activity: Turning the wheel

**Objective:** To observe the effects of static electricity generated through friction.

#### Materials
*   2 curved watch glasses
*   2 perspex rods
*   Cloth (wool or nylon)
*   Plastic rod

### Electrostatics: The Behaviour of Charges

#### 1. Fundamental Principles of Electrostatic Force
Electrostatic forces are the forces that exist between charged objects. These forces are governed by the movement of electrons between materials.

*   **Repulsion:** When objects possess the **same charge** (both positive or both negative), they push each other away. This is known as an electrostatic force of repulsion.
*   **Attraction:** When objects possess **opposite charges** (one positive and one negative), they pull each other toward one another. This is known as an electrostatic force of attraction.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0042-00.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0042-00.png)


#### 2. Factors Affecting Electrostatic Force
The strength of the electrostatic force between two objects is determined by two primary factors:
1.  **Distance:** The closer the charged objects are to each other, the stronger the electrostatic force.
2.  **Magnitude of Charge:** The more highly charged the objects are, the stronger the electrostatic force between them.

#### 3. Summary of Charge Behaviour
| Interaction | Charge Type 1 | Charge Type 2 | Result |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Repulsion | Negative | Negative | Objects push away |
| Repulsion | Positive | Positive | Objects push away |
| Attraction | Positive | Negative | Objects pull together |

# Electrostatic Discharge: From Van de Graaff to Lightning

## 1. The Van de Graaff Generator
The Van de Graaff generator is a device used to demonstrate the effects of electrostatic charge.

*   **Mechanism:** When the generator is turned on, the large metal dome at the top becomes positively charged.
*   **Discharge:** If an insulated metal sphere is brought close to the charged dome, electrons jump from the sphere to the dome, resulting in a visible spark.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0043-01.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0043-01.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0043-01.png]**
> The educational graphic is a text-based resource card with a paper background containing a hyperlink to an instructional video. The visible text reads "VISIT", "Opposites attract and like repel (video)", and includes a short URL link "bit.ly/174fSs9". It directs CAPS Physical Sciences learners to supplementary digital media demonstrating the fundamental electrostatic and magnetic rule that opposite charges or poles attract while like charges or poles repel.



> **DID YOU KNOW?**
> The fundamental idea of using friction in a machine to generate a charge dates back to the 17th century, but the generator was only invented by Robert Van de Graaff in 1929 at Princeton University.

---

## 2. Lightning as Electrostatic Discharge
Lightning is a natural, large-scale version of the electrostatic discharge seen in a Van de Graaff generator.

### The Process of Lightning Formation
1.  **Charging:** During a storm, friction between clouds and moisture causes the clouds to become electrically charged.
2.  **Charge Separation:** The bottom of the cloud (closest to the ground) becomes negatively charged, while the top of the cloud becomes positively charged.
3.  **Discharge (Earthing):** When the build-up of charge becomes too large, electrons move from the bottom of the cloud to the ground. This process is known as being "earthed."
4.  **Energy Release:** This massive transfer of energy results in:
    *   **Light:** An extremely bright flash.
    *   **Sound:** A thunder-clap, caused by the air moving rapidly as a result of the electron flow.

> **WARNING:** Lightning is extremely dangerous. If the path of the electrons to the ground passes through a person, the massive energy transfer can cause severe injury or death.

> **DID YOU KNOW?**
> South Africa has one of the highest incidences of lightning strikes in the world.

---

### Lightning Safety Precautions
*   **Seek Shelter:** The safest place to be during a lightning storm is indoors.
*   **Avoid Hazards:** Stay away from windows and metal objects.
*   **Outdoor Safety:** If you cannot get inside, avoid standing next to tall objects or metal objects, as lightning typically strikes the tallest object in the area.
*   **Vehicle Safety:** If travelling in a car, remain inside the vehicle until the storm subsides.

#### 1. Definition of Force
*   **Force:** A push or a pull on an object.
*   **Measurement:** Forces are measured in newtons ($N$).
*   **Effects:** A force can change the shape, direction, and motion of an object.
*   **Action-Reaction:** Forces act in pairs. The force acting on an object is the action, and the force the object exerts back in the opposite direction and equal in magnitude is the reaction.
*   **Net Force:** When more than one force acts on an object, the net (or resultant) force is the sum of all forces acting on the object.
*   **Representation:** Forces can be represented using a free-body diagram, where arrows indicate the direction and magnitude of the different forces.

#### 2. Types of Forces
Forces are categorized into two main groups:

*   **Contact Forces:** Act when objects are in physical contact (touching).
    *   **Friction:** The force opposing motion between two surfaces as they rub against each other.
    *   **Compression:** Two forces acting on one object, moving in opposite directions (towards each other) to compress or deform the object.
    *   **Tension:** Two forces acting on one object, moving in opposite directions (away from each other) to stretch the object.
*   **Non-contact Forces (Field Forces):** Act over a distance; objects do not need to be touching. A field is a region in space where an object with certain properties experiences a force.
    *   **Examples:** Magnetic, electrostatic, and gravitational forces.

#### 3. Mass and Weight
*   **Gravitational Force:** A force of attraction between two bodies due to their mass. It increases with mass and decreases with the distance between the bodies.
*   **Weight:** The gravitational force exerted on an object by the Earth (or another planet). Weight varies depending on location.
*   **Mass:** A measure of how much matter an object contains. Mass remains constant regardless of location.
*   **Calculation:** Weight is calculated using the formula:
    $$W = m \times g$$
    *   Where $W$ is weight.
    *   Where $m$ is mass.
    *   Where $g$ is gravitational acceleration. On Earth, $g = 9,8 m/s^2$.

# Chapter 1: Forces - Summary

## 1. Magnetism
*   **Definition of a Magnet:** A material that possesses a strong magnetic field around it.
*   **Magnetic Attraction:** Magnetic forces exist between a magnet and magnetic substances (e.g., iron, steel, cobalt, and nickel).
*   **Poles:** A magnet has two poles: a North pole and a South pole.
    *   **Law of Poles:** Opposite poles attract each other; like poles repel each other.
*   **Earth's Magnetic Field:** The Earth generates its own magnetic field. Compasses function because the needle is a magnet that aligns itself with the Earth's magnetic North.

## 2. Electrostatics
*   **Origin of Charge:** When certain materials are rubbed together, friction causes electrons to transfer from one material to another. This results in an electrostatic charge due to the gain or loss of electrons.
*   **Fundamental Properties:**
    *   A charge is a fundamental property of matter.
    *   **Electrons:** Carry a negative charge.
    *   **Protons:** Carry a positive charge.
*   **Charging Objects:**
    *   **Negative Charge:** An object that has gained electrons.
    *   **Positive Charge:** An object that has lost electrons.
*   **Electrostatic Forces:**
    *   **Attraction:** Occurs between objects with opposite charges.
    *   **Repulsion:** Occurs between objects with like charges.

## 3. Lightning
*   **Formation:** Thunderclouds become charged when water and air particles rub against each other (friction).
*   **Discharge:** A lightning strike is a massive electrostatic discharge between the thunderclouds and the ground.
*   **Safety:** Lightning is dangerous; always adhere to safety precautions during lightning storms.

# Forces: Concept Map Summary

<!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram -->

## 1. Definition and Properties of Forces
*   **Forces** act in pairs between two objects.
*   Forces can change the **speed**, **direction**, or **shape** of an object.
*   Forces are categorized into two broad groups:
    *   **Contact forces:** Forces that occur when objects are touching (e.g., friction, tension, compression).
    *   **Non-contact forces:** Forces that occur at a distance (e.g., gravitational force, magnetic force, electrostatic force).

---

## 2. Types of Forces

### A. Gravitational Force
*   **Definition:** An attractive force that exists between bodies/objects.
*   **Relationship with distance:** The force decreases as the distance between objects increases.
*   **Weight:** Gravitational force is the cause of weight.
*   **Note:** Weight is NOT constant (it depends on the gravitational pull of the body, e.g., Earth vs. Moon).

### B. Magnetic Force
*   **Definition:** A force exerted by magnets.
*   **Poles:** Magnets have two poles: **North** and **South**.
*   **Interaction:**
    *   **Attractive:** Between opposite poles.
    *   **Repulsive:** Between same poles.
*   **Magnetic Materials:** Materials that are attracted to magnets include **iron**, **steel**, **nickel**, and **cobalt**.
*   **Magnetic Field:** The region around a magnet where the magnetic force is exerted.

### C. Electrostatic Force
*   **Definition:** A force that exists between charged objects.
*   **Mechanism:** Results from the gain or loss of **electrons** during rubbing (friction).
*   **Interaction:**
    *   **Attractive:** Between opposite charges.
    *   **Repulsive:** Between like charges.
*   **Natural Example:** An electrical spark, such as **lightning** (caused by friction in thunder clouds).

---

## 3. Summary Table of Force Interactions

| Force Type | Interaction Type | Key Characteristics |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Gravitational** | Attractive | Depends on mass and distance; causes weight. |
| **Magnetic** | Attractive / Repulsive | Acts between poles; affects magnetic materials. |
| **Electrostatic** | Attractive / Repulsive | Caused by gain/loss of electrons; creates sparks. |
