### Exercise 4: Pushing and Pulling



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0049-00.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0049-00.png)


**a) Identification**
*   Identify which situation shows a push and which shows a pull.

**b) Free Body Diagram**
*   The boy is pulling the desk with a force of $70\text{ N}$.
*   There is a frictional force of $20\text{ N}$ acting against the motion.
*   Draw a free body diagram representing these forces acting on the desk.

**c) Net Force Calculation**
*   Calculate the net force ($F_{net}$) acting on the desk.
*   Formula: $F_{net} = F_{applied} - F_{friction}$
*   Calculation: $F_{net} = 70\text{ N} - 20\text{ N} = 50\text{ N}$

---

### Exercise 5: Calculating Net Force

Given two forces: $F_1 = 50\text{ N}$ and $F_2 = 80\text{ N}$.

**a) Forces acting in the same direction**
When forces act in the same direction, we add them together:
$$F_{net} = F_1 + F_2$$
$$F_{net} = 50\text{ N} + 80\text{ N} = 130\text{ N}$$

**b) Forces acting in opposite directions**
When forces act in opposite directions, we subtract the smaller force from the larger force:
$$F_{net} = F_2 - F_1$$
$$F_{net} = 80\text{ N} - 50\text{ N} = 30\text{ N}$$

### Exercises: Forces and Gravity

**9. Formula for Weight and Mass**
Write down the formula that relates the weight of an object to its mass and explain what each symbol represents.

*   **Formula:** $$w = m \cdot g$$
*   **Explanation of symbols:**
    *   $w$: Weight, measured in Newtons ($N$).
    *   $m$: Mass, measured in kilograms ($kg$).
    *   $g$: Gravitational acceleration, measured in meters per second squared ($m/s^2$).

**10. Factors Affecting Gravitational Force**
What two factors affect the gravitational force experienced between two objects? Explain the relationship.

*   **Factor 1: Mass.** The gravitational force is directly proportional to the product of the masses of the two objects. As mass increases, gravitational force increases.
*   **Factor 2: Distance.** The gravitational force is inversely proportional to the square of the distance between the centers of the two objects. As distance increases, gravitational force decreases.

---

**11. Experiment: Weight on Different Planets**
An astronaut performs an experiment to determine the relationship between mass and weight on different planets. The following table indicates his results:

| Planet | Weight (N) |
| :--- | :--- |
| Mercury | 287 |
| Venus | 710 |
| Earth | ? |
| Mars | 302 |
| Jupiter | 2076 |
| Saturn | 886 |
| Uranus | 854 |
| Neptune | 1126 |

**a) Calculate the astronaut's weight on Earth if his mass is $80\ kg$.**

*   **Formula:** $$w = m \cdot g$$
*   **Calculation:** Using the standard gravitational acceleration on Earth ($g \approx 9.8\ m/s^2$):
    $$w = 80\ kg \times 9.8\ m/s^2$$
    $$w = 784\ N$$

*   **Photo A:** Shows the magnetic field pattern between two **like poles** (e.g., North-North or South-South). The field lines push away from each other, indicating a force of **repulsion**.
*   **Photo B:** Shows the magnetic field pattern between two **opposite poles** (North-South). The field lines connect the two magnets, indicating a force of **attraction**.

### 14. Determining Overall Electric Charge

To determine the overall charge of an object, use the following calculation:
$$\text{Overall Charge} = (\text{Number of positive charges}) - (\text{Number of negative charges})$$

*   If the result is $> 0$, the object is **positively charged**.
*   If the result is $< 0$, the object is **negatively charged**.
*   If the result is $= 0$, the object is **neutral**.

| Object | Overall Charge | Why is it positive, negative or neutral? |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Blue Circle | $3 - 5 = -2$ | Negatively charged because there are more negative charges than positive charges. |
| Yellow Square | $3 - 6 = -3$ | Negatively charged because there are more negative charges than positive charges. |

# Unit 13: Forces

## Unit Overview
*   **Duration:** 2 weeks
*   **Context:** This unit builds upon Grade 8 concepts, including static electricity (friction and electrostatic force) and energy transfer in electrical systems (magnetic attraction and repulsion).
*   **Core Concepts:** Learners will explore the two main categories of forces: **contact forces** and **non-contact forces**, as well as the concept of **force fields**.
*   **Pedagogical Approach:** This unit emphasizes physical engagement. Learners should perform practical tasks such as pushing and pulling objects, pushing against buildings to experience resistance, and walking on different surfaces to experience friction.

---

## 13.1 Types of Forces (1.5 Hours)

This section focuses on identifying and classifying different types of forces through practical activities.

| Tasks | Skills | Recommendation |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Activity: What can forces do? | Group work, following instructions, doing and observing | CAPS suggested |
| Activity: Is it a push or a pull? | Accessing and recalling, identifying, sorting and classifying, describing, explaining | CAPS suggested |
| Activity: Pairs of forces | Group work, interpreting (scenarios and images), describing, explaining | CAPS suggested |
| Activity: Can forces act over a distance? | Demonstrating, observing, describing, explaining | Optional |
| Activity: Tug of war | N/A | Optional extension (PhET simulations) |

---

## 13.2 Contact Forces (1 Hour)

This section investigates the interaction between surfaces and the nature of contact forces.

| Tasks | Skills | Recommendation |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Investigation: What is the relationship between the normal force and friction? | Carrying out investigation, observing, recording, analysing, interpreting, drawing conclusion | CAPS suggested (extension) |
| Activity: Friction | N/A | Optional extension (PhET simulations) |

---

# 13.3 Field (Non-contact) Forces

## Overview of Tasks and Skills
This section covers 3.5 hours of instructional time focusing on non-contact forces.

| Tasks | Skills | Recommendation |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Investigation: Dropping objects | Group work, predicting, hypothesising, carrying out investigation, observing, recording, comparing, interpreting, drawing conclusion | CAPS suggested |
| Investigation: Relationship between mass and weight | Hypothesising, measuring, recording, drawing graph, interpreting, calculating, drawing conclusion | CAPS suggested |
| Activity: Weight and mass calculations | Calculating | CAPS suggested |
| Activity: How much would you weigh on other planets? | Measuring, calculating, interpreting information | - |
| Activity: Magnetic or non-magnetic materials | Hypothesising, observing, recording, drawing conclusion | CAPS suggested |
| Activity: Can a magnetic force act through substances? | Carrying out activity, observing, recording, describing, explaining | CAPS suggested |
| Activity: Visualising magnetic fields | Carrying out activity, observing, recording, drawing representations, communicating (graphically), describing, explaining | CAPS suggested |
| Activity: Charging objects | Group work, following instructions, observing, describing, explaining | CAPS suggested |
| Activity: Turning the wheel | Observing, describing | CAPS suggested |
| Activity: Van de Graaff generator | Observing, describing | Optional extension |

---

## Key Questions
*   What is a force?
*   What effect can a force have on an object?
*   Do forces have to be between objects which are touching?

---

## 13.1 Types of Forces
Forces are categorized by how they interact with objects. Non-contact forces (field forces) act over a distance without physical contact between the objects.

### Activity: What can forces do?
Forces can change the state of motion of an object in the following ways:
1.  **Initiating motion:** A force can cause a stationary object (like a ball) to move.
2.  **Changing direction:** A force (like a push) can change the direction of an object already in motion.
3.  **Changing speed:** A force can cause an object to slow down or stop completely.

<!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram -->

---

### Effects of Forces

Forces can have various effects on objects, including changing their speed, direction, or shape.

#### Key Concepts
*   **Motion:** Forces can cause an object to start moving, stop moving, speed up, slow down, or change direction.
*   **Deformation:** Forces can change the shape of an object. Some objects remain permanently deformed (like putty), while others return to their original shape (like a balloon).

---

### ACTIVITY: Is it a push or a pull?

The table below illustrates how different actions involve either a push or a pull and the resulting effect on the object.

| Action | Push or Pull | Effect |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| <!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram --> Kicking a ball. | Push | Kicking the ball causes the direction and speed of the ball to change. |
| <!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram --> Moulding clay. | Push | The push exerted on the clay changes the shape of the clay. |
| <!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram --> Playing with a toy wagon. | Pull | Pulling changes the motion of the wagon. |
| <!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram --> A rocket blasting off into space. | Push | Pushing force changes the motion of the rocket (accelerates it). |
| <!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram --> Exercising on a bar. | Pull | Pulling upwards changes the motion of the boy's body. |

---

### Important Note for Future Learning
Identifying the object experiencing the force and the agent causing the force is a foundational skill. This conceptual understanding is essential when applying **Newton's Laws of Motion** in higher grades.

---

### Understanding Forces and Interactions

#### 1. Identifying Forces and Agents
In any interaction, there is an object experiencing a force and an agent applying that force.
*   **Example 1:** A soccer ball being kicked. The ball is experiencing the force, and the girl's foot is the agent.
*   **Example 2:** Shaping clay. The clay is experiencing the force, and the hands are the agent.

---

#### 2. Activity: Pairs of Forces
This activity explores how forces interact when objects push or pull against each other.

**Key Observations:**
*   **Pushing against a wall:** When you push a wall, you feel the wall pushing back on your hands with equal magnitude and opposite direction.
*   **Balanced Forces:** When two learners push or pull a middle learner with equal force, the middle learner remains stationary.
*   **Unbalanced Forces:** When one learner pushes or pulls harder than the other, the middle learner moves in the direction of the stronger force.
*   **Directionality:** If two forces act in the same direction, the object moves in that direction.

**Practical Application:** A tug-of-war is an effective way to demonstrate how forces affect bodies. If one side pulls harder than the other, the rope moves toward the side with the greater force.

---

#### 3. Representing Forces
Teachers may use PhET simulations (if internet access is available) to visually represent how forces are applied and how they result in motion.

---

#### 4. Activity: Can Forces Act Over a Distance?
This activity demonstrates that forces do not always require physical contact to act on an object.

**Observations:**
*   **Magnets:** Bar magnets can move towards each other (attraction) or push each other away (repulsion) without touching.
*   **Paper Clips:** Paper clips are attracted to a bar magnet from a distance.

**Summary Questions:**
1.  **Do magnets always need to touch to exert a force?** No.
2.  **Can a magnet exert a force on an object from a distance?** Yes.
3.  **Conclusion:** A magnet exerts a force on another magnet or on magnetic materials (like paper clips) even when there is no physical contact.

<!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram -->

---

# 13.2 Contact Forces: Friction

## Investigation: The Relationship Between Normal Force and Friction
**Objective:** To determine how the normal force exerted by a surface relates to the frictional force acting on an object.

### Key Concepts
*   **Normal Force ($F_N$):** The reaction force of a surface acting on an object, perpendicular to the surface.
*   **Weight ($W$):** The gravitational force acting on an object, calculated as $W = m \times g$.
    *   Standard gravitational acceleration: $g = 9,8 \text{ m/s}^2$.
*   **Static Friction:** The maximum frictional force that must be overcome to start an object moving.
*   **Kinetic Friction:** The frictional force acting on an object while it is in motion. Note that kinetic friction is generally less than static friction.

### Methodology
1.  Use a spring balance to measure the force required to move a block across a surface.
2.  Measure the force exactly as the block **starts moving** to capture the maximum static friction.
3.  Vary the mass of the blocks to observe changes in the normal force and the resulting frictional force.

<!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram -->

### Analysis of Results
*   **Free-Body Diagram:** When an object is on a flat surface and about to move, the weight ($W$) and normal force ($F_N$) are equal in magnitude. The pulling force is equal to the static frictional force.
*   **Relationship:** As the mass ($m$) of the object increases, the weight ($W = m \times g$) increases. Consequently, the normal force ($F_N$) increases.
*   **Graphical Trend:** Plotting the frictional force against the normal force should yield a straight line passing through the origin, indicating a direct relationship.
*   **Surface Dependency:** The nature of the surface (smooth vs. rough) significantly affects the magnitude of the frictional force.

### Conclusion
1.  **Direct Proportion:** The frictional force increases as the normal force of the object increases.
2.  **Motion Dynamics:** Once the block is in motion at a constant speed, the measured friction decreases compared to the initial static friction required to start the movement.

---

### 13.3 Field (Non-contact) Forces

Field forces are forces that act over a distance without physical contact between objects.

#### Gravitational Force
Gravity is defined as the gravitational force of attraction between two objects with mass. 
*   **Planetary Gravity:** When referring to "gravity" in a general sense, we are usually discussing the gravitational attraction between a celestial body (like the Earth) and other objects.
*   **Newton's Third Law Application:** The force exerted by the Earth on an object is equal in magnitude to the force exerted by the object on the Earth, but they act in opposite directions. Because the Earth has a significantly larger mass, its acceleration is negligible, which is why the Earth does not move noticeably when objects fall toward it.

#### Key Relationships
Using simulations (such as PhET), we can observe the following qualitative relationships regarding gravitational force:
1.  **Mass:** The gravitational force between two objects **increases** as the mass of the objects increases.
2.  **Distance:** The gravitational force between two objects **decreases** as the distance between the objects increases.

---

### Investigation: Dropping Objects

#### Concept: Falling Objects
A common misconception among learners is that heavier objects fall faster than lighter objects. This investigation is designed to challenge this preconception.

**Experimental Procedure:**
1.  Work in pairs.
2.  Drop a whole apple and half an apple from the same height at the same time.
3.  Experiment with balls of different masses (but the same size).
4.  Experiment with balls of the same mass (but different volumes).

**Teacher's Note:**
*   When air resistance is negligible, objects of different masses will hit the ground at the same time.
*   Do not lead learners to the correct answer immediately; allow them to discover the results through the experimental process.

<!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram -->

---

### Extension Activity: Friction
*   **Static Friction:** The force that resists the initiation of sliding motion between two surfaces that are in contact and at rest.
*   **Moving (Kinetic) Friction:** Once an object begins to move, the friction force typically decreases.
*   **Activity:** Use play dough to form balls and compress them in your hands to explore physical properties and forces.

---

### Investigation: Falling Objects

#### Experimental Procedure
*   **Method:** Drop objects simultaneously from the same height.
*   **Observation:** Listen for the number of sounds upon impact. If two sounds are heard, the objects did not hit the floor at the same time.
*   **Repetition:** Repeat the investigation multiple times to ensure accuracy and overcome preconceptions.
*   **Safety Tip:** If using apples, cut them in half ahead of time.

#### Demonstration: Air Resistance
*   **Experiment:** Drop a hammer and a feather simultaneously.
*   **Observation:** The hammer lands first.
*   **Explanation:** Air resistance acts on the feather, slowing its fall. In a vacuum (where there is no air resistance), both objects would fall at the same rate and hit the floor at the same time.

---

#### Results and Observations
1.  **Height:** The height from which objects are dropped.
2.  **Object Properties:** The mass and volume of the objects being dropped.

#### Evaluation
Learners should reflect on the experiment. Common challenges include:
*   Difficulty in releasing objects at the exact same time.
*   The need for a greater drop height to observe results more clearly.
*   The interference of air resistance.

#### Conclusion
*   Objects of different masses or volumes fall at the same rate if air resistance is ignored.
*   All objects accelerate at the same rate due to gravity.

---

#### Advanced Concepts: Physics Principles

**1. Newton's Second Law of Motion**
The net force ($F$) exerted on an object is defined by the equation:
$$F = m \cdot a$$
Where:
*   $F$ = Net force (Newtons)
*   $m$ = Mass of the object (kg)
*   $a$ = Acceleration produced by the net force ($m/s^2$)

**2. Universal Law of Gravitation**
The Earth's gravitational force pulls on an object with a force proportional to the mass of the object and the mass of the Earth. Because the mass of the Earth is constant, the acceleration of a falling object is independent of its own mass.

**3. Real-world Factors (Air Resistance)**
In a real-world environment, the net force acting on a falling object is:
$$F_{net} = F_{gravity} - F_{air\_resistance}$$
Because the feather is much lighter than the hammer, the force of air resistance has a significantly larger impact on the feather's acceleration compared to the hammer.

---

#### Questions and Answers

1.  **Did the objects land at the same time?** They should have landed at the same (or very close to the same) time.
2.  **What happens in an ideal situation?** In an ideal situation (a vacuum), all objects dropped from the same height will land at the same time.
3.  **Why do objects fall?** The Earth's gravitational force causes each object to accelerate at the same rate every second, regardless of its mass or volume.

<!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram -->

---

### Air Resistance and Gravity

When an object falls, the net force acting on it determines its acceleration. If air resistance is present, the net force is reduced, causing the object to experience a smaller acceleration towards the ground and fall more slowly.

#### Advanced Teacher's Note: Air Resistance
Air resistance is a drag force that acts to slow an object down. The magnitude of this force depends on:
1. The velocity of the falling object squared ($v^2$).
2. The surface area of the falling object.
3. The density of the fluid (in this case, air) through which it is falling.

*   **Light objects:** Objects like feathers or thin sheets of paper are significantly slowed by air resistance because their gravitational force is very small compared to the drag force.
*   **Large objects:** Large surface areas (like an open parachute) experience greater air resistance, which significantly reduces the falling speed.

---

### Mass vs. Weight

It is essential to distinguish between mass and weight in scientific contexts:

*   **Mass:** A measure of the amount of matter in an object. It remains constant regardless of your position in space. It is measured in kilograms ($kg$).
*   **Weight:** A force experienced by an object due to gravity. On Earth, it is the force of attraction towards the centre of the Earth. Because it is a force, it is measured in Newtons ($N$).

**Common Misconception:** Saying "My weight increased by 2 kg" is scientifically incorrect. Kilograms measure mass. If you gain matter, your *mass* has increased by 2 kg.

**Gravitational Influence:** The gravitational force depends on the size of the planet. The Earth is much larger than the Moon; therefore, the gravitational force (weight) exerted on an object on Earth will be greater than the force exerted on the same object on the Moon.

---

### Investigation: The Relationship Between Mass and Weight

**Hypothesis:** (Learner-dependent answer)

**Materials and Apparatus:**
*   Mass meter (e.g., kitchen scales or electronic scales).
*   Various mass pieces.

<!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram -->

**Results:**
The following table demonstrates the relationship between mass and weight:

| Mass (kg) | Weight (N) |
| :--- | :--- |
| 0.5 | 4.8 |
| 1 | 9.8 |
| 1.5 | 14.7 |
| 2 | 19.6 |

**Analysis:**
1.  **Independent Variable:** Mass (plotted on the x-axis).
2.  **Dependent Variable:** Weight (plotted on the y-axis).
3.  **Gradient:** The gradient of the graph of weight versus mass is approximately $9.8$. This represents the gravitational field strength of the Earth ($g \approx 9.8 \, m/s^2$).

---

### Calculating the Gradient of a Straight Line

To determine the gradient of a straight line from a graph, follow these steps:
1. Choose two coordinates on the line: $(x_1; y_1)$ and $(x_2; y_2)$.
2. Use the gradient formula:
   $$\text{gradient} = \frac{\text{rise}}{\text{run}} = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}$$

**Worked Example:**
Using coordinates $(0,5; 4,8)$ and $(1; 9,8)$:
$$\text{gradient} = \frac{9,8 - 4,8}{1 - 0,5} = 9,8$$

*Note: The gradient of this graph represents the gravitational acceleration on Earth. If your results are not exactly 9,8, ensure your graph is plotted correctly and your spring balances are calibrated.*

---

### Conclusion
The weight of an object is directly proportional to the object's mass.

---

### ACTIVITY: Weight and Mass Calculations
1. $\text{Weight} = 1485 \times 9,8 = 14553\text{ N}$
2. $50\text{ kg}$ is the mass of an object and is independent of position.
3. $\text{Weight on Moon} = 78 \times 1,6 = 124,8\text{ N}$ (Sam is incorrect).
4. $\text{Mass} = 220\text{ g} = 0,22\text{ kg}$
   * $\text{Weight on Earth} = 0,22 \times 9,8 = 2,156\text{ N}$
   * $\text{Weight on Moon} = 0,22 \times 1,6 = 0,352\text{ N}$
5. $\text{Mass} = \frac{1340}{1,6} = 837,5\text{ kg}$

---

### ACTIVITY: How much would you weigh on other planets?
Mass remains constant regardless of location, but weight varies based on the gravitational field strength ($g$) of the planet.

**Example calculations for a $50\text{ kg}$ learner:**

| Planet | Your mass (kg) | Value of $g$ ($\text{m/s}^2$) | Your weight (N) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Earth | 50 | 9,8 | 490 |
| Mercury | 50 | 3,6 | 180 |
| Venus | 50 | 8,8 | 440 |
| Mars | 50 | 3,8 | 190 |
| Jupiter | 50 | 26 | 1300 |

<!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram -->

### A Note on Weightlessness

**Definition:** Weightlessness refers to the sensation of weight, or the lack thereof, rather than a change in actual weight. It is a feeling experienced when there are no external contact forces (pushes or pulls from objects touching you) acting upon a person.

**Key Concepts:**
*   **Weight:** The force of gravitational attraction experienced by an object.
*   **Free-fall:** A state where an object experiences weightlessness because it is falling at the same rate as its surroundings. Even in free-fall, the object still experiences gravitational attraction.
*   **Astronauts in Orbit:** A common misconception is that there is no gravity in space. In reality, in low Earth orbit, gravity is still at approximately $90\%$ of its surface strength. Astronauts float because they and their spacecraft are in a state of constant free-fall, moving at an orbital velocity that prevents them from hitting the Earth.

### Magnetic Forces

#### INVESTIGATION: Magnetic or non-magnetic materials

**Hypothesis**
Learner-dependent. Example: "Only some materials are magnetic."

**Method**
Observe which materials are attracted to a magnet. Note that non-metals are not attracted, and some metals (like copper) are also not attracted.

**Results**

| Material | Magnetic (YES/NO) |
| :--- | :--- |
| Paper | NO |
| Wood | NO |
| Plastic | NO |
| Iron | YES |
| Aluminium | NO |
| Steel | YES |

<!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram -->

---

### Material Properties and Magnetism

| Material | Magnetic |
| :--- | :--- |
| Copper | NO |

**Conclusion:** Not all materials are magnetic. Only some metals, such as iron, are magnetic.

### ACTIVITY: Can a magnetic force act through substances? (LB page 212)

Magnetic forces are **non-contact forces** and can act over a distance. However, normal magnets do not have infinitely strong magnetic fields. The further an object is from the magnet, the weaker the force experienced.

If an object is placed between a magnet and a metal, and the metal is too thick, the metal may be too far away from the magnet to experience a strong enough force. This lack of attraction is due to the strength of the magnetic field decreasing with distance, rather than the material "blocking" the force.

#### Instructions
1. Two like poles repel each other (a "pushing" force).
2. Two like poles repel each other (a "pushing" force).
3. Two opposite poles attract each other (a "pulling" force).
4. Magnets can act through most substances, provided they are thin enough.
5. The distance between the magnet and the object (e.g., paper clips) is the primary factor affecting attraction. A thick piece of wood may keep the paper clips far enough away from the magnet to make the attraction too weak to pick them up.

#### Questions
1. **Which material prevented the magnet from picking up the paper clips?** The thick piece of wood.
2. **How does magnetic force behave over distance?** It acts over a distance; it is strongest closer to the magnet and becomes weaker as the distance increases.

### ACTIVITY: Visualising magnetic fields (LB page 213)

Iron filings can be used to map the magnetic field around a magnet.

*   **Note:** The iron filings show a two-dimensional view of the field, but the magnetic field actually exists all around the magnet in three dimensions.

<!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram -->

#### Instructions
Observe the pattern formed by the iron filings around a bar magnet.

---

### Magnetic Field Patterns

Magnetic field lines represent the direction and strength of a magnetic field. The patterns formed by iron filings around magnets demonstrate how magnetic poles interact.

#### 1. Attraction between Opposite Poles
When a North pole ($N$) and a South pole ($S$) are placed near each other, the magnetic field lines connect the two poles, pulling them together.
<!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram -->
*The pattern between two opposite poles attracting.*

#### 2. Repulsion between Like Poles
When two like poles (either two North poles or two South poles) are placed near each other, the magnetic field lines push away from each other, creating a region where the field strength is very low.
<!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram -->
*The pattern between two like poles repelling.*

### Electrostatic Forces

Electrostatic forces are forces of attraction or repulsion between stationary electric charges. 

**Note to Learners:**
Even though experiments regarding electrostatic forces were covered in Grade 8, it is essential to revisit them. Practical engagement with these experiments is necessary to build a foundational understanding of how advanced apparatus, such as the **electroscope** and **Van de Graaff generators**, function.

<!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram -->

---

### ACTIVITY: Charging Objects

This activity explores static electricity. You may use balloons, plastic combs, or pieces of paper. Note that results may vary depending on hair type and environmental conditions.

#### Instructions & Observations
1. **Initial State:** Nothing happens.
2. **Interaction:** Hair should "rise" and stick to the balloon.
3. **Neutral State:** Nothing happens.
4. **Interaction:** Pieces of paper stick to the glass rod.
5. **Interaction:** A stream of water bends towards the glass rod.

#### Key Concepts: Atomic Charges
*   **Protons:** Carry a positive charge ($+$).
*   **Electrons:** Carry a negative charge ($-$). They are positioned in the space around the nucleus.
*   **Neutrons:** Carry no charge; they are neutral.

#### Questions & Explanations
1. **How is the object charged?** By rubbing it vigorously (e.g., with a balloon).
2. **Transfer of Charge:** Friction causes electrons to transfer from the glass rod to the knitted fabric. The glass rod loses electrons and becomes **positively charged**, while the wool gains electrons and becomes **negatively charged**.
3. **Attraction:** Water contains both positive and negative charges. When a positively charged rod is brought near, the negative charges in the water are attracted to the rod, causing the stream to bend.



![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0068-00.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0068-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0068-00.png]**
> 1. A black and white illustration of a Lewis structure of an atom.

### ACTIVITY: Turning the Wheel

This demonstration illustrates the fundamental laws of electrostatics:
*   **Like charges repel** each other.
*   **Unlike charges attract** each other.

#### Demonstration Notes
*   **Environmental Factors:** This activity works best on a dry day. Humidity can cause charges to dissipate (earth) quickly.
*   **Repulsion:** When two rods with like charges are brought near each other, the second rod will "push" the first rod, causing it to move in a circle.
*   **Attraction:** When rods have opposite charges, the second rod will "pull" the first rod around in a circle.
*   **Maintenance:** You may need to rub the perspex rod again between attempts, as the static charge dissipates over time.

![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0068-02.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr9_Forces.pdf-0068-02.png)

### Electrostatics: Interaction of Charged Objects

When different materials are rubbed together, electrons are transferred, resulting in static charges.

*   **Perspex Rods:** When rubbed with a cloth, electrons are transferred from the perspex to the cloth. This leaves the perspex rod with a **positive charge**.
*   **Plastic Rods:** When rubbed with a cloth, electrons are transferred from the cloth to the plastic rod. This leaves the plastic rod with a **negative charge**.

#### Observed Interactions
| Scenario | Interaction | Result |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Two Perspex rods | Like charges repel | The first rod moves away from the second; the watch glass turns. |
| Perspex and Plastic rods | Opposite charges attract | The first rod moves towards the plastic rod; the watch glass turns towards the plastic rod. |
| Rod and Paper | Charged object and neutral object | The pieces of paper are attracted to the rod. |

---

### Extension Activity: Van de Graaff Generator
The Van de Graaff generator is a tool used to demonstrate static electricity and the formation of sparks. It serves as a practical model to explain the physics behind natural phenomena such as lightning.

### Revision Summary: Forces and Motion

#### 1. Key Terminology
*   **Force:** A push or a pull acting on an object.
*   **Field:** A region in space where an object experiences a force (e.g., gravitational or electrostatic field).
*   **Free-fall:** Motion under the influence of gravity only.
*   **Poles:** Regions of a magnet where the magnetic force is strongest.
*   **Charge:** A property of matter that causes it to experience a force when placed in an electromagnetic field.

#### 2. True or False Concepts
*   **Motion:** A force can cause a motionless object to move.
*   **Friction:** Friction is a force that opposes motion; it slows down or stops an object by the interaction of surfaces rubbing against each other.
*   **Lightning:** Lightning is a large-scale application of electrostatic force.

#### 3. Force Diagrams and Calculations
When representing forces in a diagram:
*   **Push vs. Pull:** A push acts away from the object, while a pull acts towards the source of the force.
*   **Vector Representation:** 
    *   Use a dot to represent the object.
    *   Use arrows to represent the direction of the force.
    *   The length of the arrow represents the **magnitude** of the force.

**Example Calculation:**
If a force of $50\text{ N}$ is applied to the right, the arrow must be drawn pointing to the right with a length proportional to the value of $50\text{ N}$.

---

### 6. Effects of Forces
Forces can cause the following changes to an object:
*   Change in motion (moving faster or slower).
*   Change in direction.
*   Change in shape.

### 7. Types of Forces
<!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram -->
*   **Compression forces:** Forces applied to squeeze or compress an object (Image B).
*   **Tension forces:** Forces applied to stretch an object (Image C).

### 8. Mass vs. Weight
| Feature | Mass | Weight |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Definition** | The amount of matter an object is made of; it remains constant regardless of location. | The gravitational force exerted on an object by a planet or moon; it varies based on gravitational strength. |
| **Units** | kilograms (kg) | newtons (N) |

### 9. Calculating Weight
The formula to calculate weight is:
$$W = m \times g$$

Where:
*   $W$ = weight of the object in Newtons (N)
*   $m$ = mass of the object in kilograms (kg)
*   $g$ = gravitational acceleration in meters per second squared ($\text{m/s}^2$)

### 10. Factors Affecting Gravitational Force
*   **Distance:** The closer two objects are, the stronger the gravitational force between them.
*   **Mass:** The greater the mass of the objects, the stronger the gravitational force between them.

### 11. Worked Example: Weight Calculation
**a) Calculation:**
Given a mass of $80\text{ kg}$ and gravitational acceleration of $9,8\text{ m/s}^2$:
$$W = m \times g$$
$$W = 80 \times 9,8$$
$$W = 784\text{ N}$$

**b) Graphing Requirements:**
When drawing a bar graph, ensure the following criteria are met:
*   Gaps between bars.
*   Clear heading.
*   Labeled x-axis.
*   Labeled y-axis with units.
*   Appropriate vertical scale.
*   Accurate plotting.

**c) Planetary Weight:**
An object's weight is smallest on Mercury because Mercury has the smallest mass of the planets.

### 13. Magnetic Fields
<!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram -->
*   **Attraction:** Magnetic fields between two opposite poles (North and South) pull towards each other.
*   **Repulsion:** Magnetic fields between two like poles (North and North, or South and South) push away from each other.

---

### 14. Calculating and Explaining Electric Charge

To determine the overall charge of an object, we sum the number of positive charges ($+$) and negative charges ($-$). 
*   **Neutral:** Equal number of positive and negative charges (Sum = $0$).
*   **Negative:** More negative charges than positive charges (Sum $< 0$).
*   **Positive:** More positive charges than negative charges (Sum $> 0$).

| Object | Overall Charge Calculation | Explanation |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| <!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram --> | $4 + (-4) = 0$ | Neutral: Equal numbers of positive and negative charges. |
| <!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram --> | $3 + (-6) = -3$ | Negatively charged: There are $3$ more negative than positive charges. |
| <!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram --> | $7 + (-3) = 4$ | Positively charged: There are $4$ more positive charges than negative charges. |

---

### 15. Static Electricity Concepts
*   **a)** Electrons are transferred from the jersey to the balloon. Protons are not transferred. Because electrons are negatively charged, the balloon acquires a negative charge.
*   **b)** The process of transferring charge through rubbing is called **friction**.
*   **c)** The force acting between the charged objects is the **electrostatic force**.
*   **d)** The nature of the force between the oppositely charged jersey and balloon is **attractive**.

---

### 16. Van de Graaff Generator
When girls touch the hollow dome of a Van de Graaff generator:
1.  The dome is positively charged.
2.  Electrons are transferred from the girls' bodies to the dome to discharge it.
3.  This causes the girls' bodies and hair to become positively charged.
4.  Because like charges repel, the individual hair strands push away from each other, causing the hair to stand up (rise).

---

### 17. Lightning Formation
*   **Cause:** Friction between water and air particles in clouds causes a build-up of negative charge.
*   **Discharge:** When there is a large excess of charge, it moves through the air to the ground.
*   **Energy Release:** The discharge creates a bright spark, releasing energy in the form of heat, light, and sound. This phenomenon is known as **lightning**.

---

### 18. Lightning Safety
Playing outside during a lightning storm is dangerous. Specifically, standing underneath a tree is extremely hazardous because lightning tends to strike tall objects. Being near a tree puts a person at high risk of being shocked by the discharge.
